Full text

Abstract

Racial discrimination is a pervasive adolescent stressor, yet hierarchical-dimensional frameworks such as HiTOP have been validated in samples underrepresenting minoritized youth, and daily-level evidence on discrimination and youth mental health comes almost entirely from U.S. samples and narrow symptom sets. In a preregistered 29-day daily-diary study of 310 ethnoracially minoritized adolescents (ages 14-21) in Germany (8,054 person-days), we evaluated a HiTOP-aligned daily symptom inventory and examined how daily racial discrimination and general stress related to symptom fluctuations. Six factors replicated within persons, while the between-person structure resolved into three broader spectra. Discrimination and general stress both predicted same-day symptom elevations; next-day associations were largely absent or indicated recovery. Beyond general stress, discrimination showed incremental same-day elevations in internalizing, antagonism, disinhibition, and psychoticism, strongest for antagonism. Discrimination operated as an acute, identity-relevant stressor not reducible to general daily stress.

Structure and Dynamics of Daily Psychopathology in Ethnoracially Minoritized Youth: Links to Discrimination and Stress

Ethnic-racial discrimination is among the most consequential stressors adolescents face, yet it remains peripheral to theoretical models of how psychopathology emerges and unfolds in daily life. Two limitations in the literature compound each other. First, intensive longitudinal evidence linking daily discrimination to youth mental health comes almost exclusively from U.S. samples and narrow symptom sets, leaving open whether these dynamics generalize to other national contexts and to the broader space of psychopathology. Second, the multidimensional frameworks increasingly used to map that space, most prominently the Hierarchical Taxonomy of Psychopathology (HiTOP; Kotov et al., 2017 Kotov et al., , 2021)), have been validated predominantly in samples that underrepresent ethnoracially minoritized youth. Instruments built on these taxonomies may therefore misread discrimination-related adaptations as psychopathology: vigilance toward threat, social withdrawal, and institutional mistrust can resemble symptom content that was never validated with this population in view (Rodriguez-Seijas et al., 2023). The present study addresses both limitations with a 29-day daily-diary study of ethnoracially minoritized adolescents in Germany, examining how daily discrimination relates to withinperson fluctuations across a hierarchically organized set of symptom dimensions.

Adolescence is a period of heightened sensitivity to social stress, and discrimination is among its most pervasive forms (Benner et al., 2018; Cave et al., 2020). Survey studies, largely from the United States, estimated that ethnoracially minoritized youth encounter discrimination once or twice a week in schools, public spaces, and institutional settings (Ortega-Williams et al., 2022; Steketee et al., 2021; Yip, 2018). Cumulatively, these experiences were associated with elevated risk for depression, anxiety, posttraumatic stress, and sleep disturbance, with compounding effects at intersecting marginalized identities (Polanco-Roman et al., 2024; Trent et al., 2019). Germany offers an informative and understudied context: the proportion of schoolaged children of immigrant descent rose from 9.6% in 2005 to 39.1% in 2019 and forms a numerical majority in several metropolitan areas (Blossfeld et al., 2016; German Federal Statistical Office, 2021). In this population, frequent discrimination in schools, public spaces, and police interactions has been documented, and perceived exclusion, that is, being denied recognition as "truly German," predicted depression, anxiety, and diminished institutional trust (DeZIM, 2025). Daily-level evidence on discrimination and adolescent mental health in Germany is nonetheless essentially absent.

Addressing this gap requires treating discrimination experiences as conceptually distinct from general daily stress rather than as one entry in a broader inventory of adolescent stressors.

Whereas general stressors involve situational burden or challenge, discrimination carries an additional, group-based meaning, because the negative treatment is attributed to ethnic-racial group membership (Jones, 2000; Yip et al., 2020). Two theoretical accounts explain why this attribution matters. Social identity theory holds that group memberships form part of the selfconcept, so that threats to a valued group also threaten the self (Tajfel & Turner, 1979). The rejection-identification model specifies that attributing negative treatment to prejudice signals rejection by the dominant group, with adverse consequences for well-being (Branscombe et al., 1999). Discrimination experiences and general stressors may therefore both affect daily psychopathology, but only discrimination is theorized to carry identity-relevant threat.

According to stress process theory, chronic strains and acute daily stressors reinforce each other in shaping mental health: ongoing strain sensitizes individuals to everyday stressors, and recurrent daily stressors entrench chronic strain (Lazarus & Folkman, 1984; Pearlin, 1989). Discrimination operates at both levels, as a chronic strain and as a recurrent daily stressor (Ong et al., 2009). Because it targets salient social identities, it is not merely an external burden but a threat to self-worth and belonging, which may intensify this reciprocal escalation. Capturing these dynamics requires designs that resolve experience at the timescale on which it occurs, in line with current views of psychopathology as a dynamic system of interacting symptoms and person-context processes (Fried, 2022; Hamaker & Wichers, 2017; Wright et al., 2023). A recent systematic review (Civitillo & Jugert, 2024) and meta-analysis (Wang et al., 2026) found robust concurrent within-person associations between daily discrimination and youth mental health, chiefly internalizing symptoms and sleep problems, whereas evidence on next-day carryover was equivocal; all but one of the reviewed studies, however, were conducted in the United States.

The scarce non-U.S. evidence is consistent with such day-level dynamics but does not test them directly. A German study of Arabic-and Turkish-heritage adolescents found that school-based discrimination predicted same-day declines in cognitive engagement and next-day declines in emotional engagement (Civitillo et al., 2024), and research with asylum-seeking youth documented about five discriminatory events per two-week period (Müller et al., 2021). Neither study examined within-person associations between daily discrimination and youth psychopathology.

In parallel with the growth of intensive longitudinal designs, psychopathology research has converged on hierarchical, dimensional frameworks that organize symptoms into transdiagnostic spectra and address well-documented limitations of categorical nosologies (Kotov et al., 2017 (Kotov et al., , 2021)). Such frameworks are particularly suited to discrimination research.

Among Black Americans and Black individuals of Caribbean descent, racial discrimination was more strongly related to broadband internalizing and externalizing spectra than to specific PREPRINT: DISCRIMINATION AND DAILY PSYCHOPATHOLOGY IN YOUTH 6 diagnoses (Rodriguez-Seijas et al., 2015), and social stress in adolescence predicted internalizing and externalizing domains rather than any specific disorder over a 20-year follow-up (Conway et al., 2018). Until recently, however, these frameworks rested on between-person, cross-sectional data, which assume that within-person symptom covariation mirrors between-person structure, an assumption Molenaar (2004 The first is whether this structure extends to youth. In a U.S. community sample aged 9 to 17, Hankin et al. (2026) recovered three broadband between-person spectra (internalizing, disinhibited externalizing, and personality dysregulation) that held largely consistently across informants, gender, pubertal status, and an 18-month interval. The sample, however, was 70% White, and the authors cautioned against generalizing across cultures or languages. The second question is whether the structure holds for ethnoracially minoritized youth outside the United States. Its stakes go beyond psychometrics, because chronic exposure to discrimination may change how symptoms are felt, displayed, and reported (Rodriguez-Seijas et al., 2023). Elevated detachment may partly reflect withdrawal as a protective response to hostile environments, and suspiciousness may reflect warranted mistrust of institutions with documented histories of bias (Kingston et al., 2023; Whaley, 2001). Treating such responses as straightforward indicators of pathology risks pathologizing adaptation, so it is essential to first establish whether a hierarchical daily measure functions coherently in this population. Coherent functioning has three testable aspects: the daily items should reproduce the expected hierarchical organization of symptom domains within and between persons (structural validity), individuals' average daily scores should converge with established trait inventories of the same constructs (between-person convergent validity), and day-to-day fluctuations should track same-day affect and functioning in theoretically expected ways (within-person convergent validity).

The Present Study

Treating discrimination as a dynamic daily stressor rather than a static exposure requires a measurement model spanning multiple symptom domains at multiple levels of generality, and a design that resolves stressor and symptom at the daily timescale. Such a design allows discrimination to be related to symptoms on the day it occurs and the day after, and to be distinguished from general daily stress. We pursued two aims.

The first aim was to evaluate the psychometric properties of a daily-diary adaptation of the Wright et al. (2025) hierarchical symptom inventory in ethnoracially minoritized adolescents (ages 14-21) in Germany. Because these analyses evaluate a measurement model in a new population rather than test directional predictions, we framed them as exploratory research questions. We first asked which hierarchical structure of psychopathology emerges within and between persons (RQ1.1). Extending the five-domain inventory of Wright et al. (2025) by psychoticism items, we expected six domains-Internalizing, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition, anankastia, and psychoticism-to vary meaningfully from day to day and to covary similarly at both levels. We then asked to what extent individuals' average daily psychopathology levels align with established baseline inventories (PID-5, YSR; RQ1.2), anticipating convergence, and to what extent within-person fluctuations in daily psychopathology covary with daily affect, well-being, and perceived emotion regulation efficacy (RQ1.3), expecting higher negative affect and lower regulation efficacy on days with elevated symptoms.

The second aim was to test preregistered, theory-derived hypotheses about how daily ethnic-racial discrimination-directly and vicariously experienced, modeled both dichotomously and by perceived severity-and general stressors relate to symptom fluctuations, and to isolate discrimination's contribution beyond general daily stress. Concurrently, we hypothesized that youth report elevated psychopathology on days with direct or vicarious discrimination relative to days without (H1.1), on days with more severe discrimination experiences (H1.2), and on days with higher general stress (H1.3). Over time, we hypothesized that discrimination (H2.1), discrimination severity (H2.2), and general stress (H2.3) on day t predict elevated psychopathology on day t + 1, controlling for day-t psychopathology. At the between-person level, we hypothesized that youth with more frequent or more severe discrimination or general stress report higher average psychopathology (H3).

Method

Data, materials, code, and online resources.

De-identified data, study materials (including the full codebook with all item wordings), and analysis scripts are available on OSF (https://osf.io/vptus) and will be permanently archived with a DOI on Zenodo upon acceptance.

Reporting.

We report how we determined our sample size, all data exclusions, all manipulations, and all measures in the study. The study involved no experimental manipulations.

The daily-diary protocol assessed a broader set of constructs than addressed here, and the complete data-collection design included a four-day diary extension and a 6-day ecological momentary assessment phase, neither of which is analyzed in the present manuscript; all collected measures are listed in the OSF codebook (https://osf.io/dqv8f/).. Participants. Participants were adolescents and young adults aged 14-21 residing in Germany who had migrated themselves or had at least one parent who had migrated; inclusion criteria were confirmed by telephone. The sample comprised 310 participants (M = 17.49 years, SD = 1.80), of whom 178 (57.42%) identified as female, 126 (40.65%) as male, and 3 as nonbinary; 2 preferred not to answer and 1 reported another gender identity. Most were still in school (69.68%), 20.32% were enrolled at university, and most described their financial situation as average (41.61%) or good (43.23%). Most participants (73.55%, n = 228) were born in Germany to one or both migrant parents; the remainder had migrated themselves, predominantly from the Middle East or West Asia (13.87 %, n = 43, mostly Syria) or South-East Europe (8.71%, n = 27; e.g., Poland, Ukraine, Greece). Participants' reports of parental countries of origin were grouped into regions. The most frequently represented regions were South-East Europe (32.67%, n = 99), the Middle East and West Asia (32.34%, n = 98), and North Africa (16.17%, n = 49). Percentages refer to 303 regional classifications, with participants potentially represented in more than one region due to differences in their parents' countries of origin.

Nearly half identified as Muslim (47.42%), 30.97% as Christian, and 13.23% as atheist or nonreligious, while the remainder indentified with other specific religious or spiritual groups.

Power Considerations. Because the dataset was designed as a resource for multiple research questions, the sample size target prioritized estimation precision over conventional statistical power. Following Schönbrodt and Perugini (2013), we targeted a minimum of N = 250 for stable correlation estimates (±.10 precision) and oversampled to N = 300 ethnoracially minoritized youth to account for attrition (the larger data collection, targeting N = 450, also included participants without migration history).

Recruitment. Consistent with a dimensional, transdiagnostic framework, participants were recruited to span a broad range of symptom expression, including adolescents engaged and not engaged in clinical services. Recruitment drew on community networks, online platforms, and flyers; of 104 organizations contacted, 57 collaborated, most commonly schools (n = 40) and local clubs and associations (n = 17). Eligibility required attendance at in-person baseline and closing sessions. To reduce topic-related self-selection, the study was advertised as an investigation of adolescents' everyday experiences; during informed consent, participants (and parents of minors) were fully informed about all study content, including questions about discrimination and migration history.

Procedure. The study used an intensive longitudinal design comprising a baseline assessment and a 29-day daily-diary phase, preceded by a pilot test of feasibility, notification timing, usability, and response logging. Interested adolescents completed an online screening survey (age, gender, migration history) and a follow-up call confirming eligibility, then attended an in-person baseline session where they completed sociodemographic and dispositional questionnaires at a computer station, and installed the m-Path app on their personal phones, generating an anonymous participant ID. The diary phase began immediately after baseline and ran for 28 consecutive days, plus one optional baseline-evening survey (maximum 29 entries).

Each evening at 19:30, participants received a notification to complete a ~7-minute survey on daily discrimination, stressors, coping, and well-being; surveys remained open until 23:30, with one reminder after 60 minutes and sequential item completion required. Weekly phone check-ins addressed technical issues, encouraged compliance, verified discrimination reports, and confirmed continued residence in Germany. Participants completed an in-person debriefing session, with compensation following a tiered structure based on compliance (maximum €125) 1.

Measures

Measurement of Ethnoracial Minoritization Status.

Minoritization status was operationalized via migration history, defined as having migrated to Germany oneself or having at least one parent who had migrated (Starker et al., 2021). Status was assessed by online screening and confirmed in a follow-up phone call, with the phone confirmation taking precedence over discrepant screening responses. Grandparental migration alone did not qualify participants for inclusion. 2

Baseline Measures Personality Pathology. Adolescents completed the German Modified Personality Inventory for DSM-5, Brief Form Plus (PID5BF+M; Bach et al., 2020), a 36-item inventory covering 18 facets (two items each) that combine into six domain scales: Negative Affectivity, 1 Compensation was tiered by compliance across the daily diary and a 6-day ecological momentary assessment phase embedded in the diary period, which is not analyzed here (details on OSF: https://osf.io/dqv8f/). 2 Consistent with accounts of racialization in Germany (Ball et al., 2022; DeZIM, 2023; German Federal Government, 2017), participants whose migration background was from Western European countries (France, United Kingdom, Netherlands, Belgium, Luxembourg, Austria, Switzerland, Liechtenstein) were excluded at screening, as they are less likely to be racialized as "other" in Germany and their discrimination experiences differ from those of systemically racialized groups.

Detachment, Antagonism, Disinhibition, Psychoticism, and Anankastia (six items per domain).

Response options ranged from 1 (very false/often false) to 4 (very true/often true).

Psychopathology. Behavioral problems were assessed with the Youth Self-Report (YSR/11-18R; Döpfner et al., 2014). To reduce participant burden, we administered 90 of the 112 items, retaining all items required to score the DSM-oriented Depressive Problems, Anxiety Problems, Conduct Problems, Oppositional Defiant Problems, and Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Problems scales and omitting items that belong to no subscale as well as the remaining "other problems" and "physical problems" items. Response options ranged from 0 (applies not at all) to 2 (fully applies).

Daily Assessed Constructs.

Daily Psychopathology. Daily psychopathology was assessed with an 18-item inventory adapted from Wright et al. (2025), whose 15-item short form yielded a hierarchical multilevel structure spanning internalizing, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition, and anankastia in U.S. adults (Mage = 28, SD = 6.38). We added three indicators tapping psychoticism-related content (unusual beliefs and experiences, distorted thinking, perceptual dysregulation; e.g.: "… I saw connections between events that others do not see"), yielding six domains of three items each.

Items were presented with the stem "Today…" and rated on a continuous slider from 0 (not at all) to 10 (very much); domain scores were computed as the mean of the three contributing items.

Negative and Positive Affect. Affect was assessed with seven items rated from 0 (does not apply at all) to 10 (fully applies), anchored to a specific social interaction that day-either the reported discrimination incident (direct or vicarious) or, on days without such an incident, another interaction participants selected. Negative affect comprised five items (angry, sad, PREPRINT: DISCRIMINATION AND DAILY PSYCHOPATHOLOGY IN YOUTH 13 anxious, ashamed, stressed) and positive affect two (content, empowered); scores were computed as domain means per person-day.

Well-Being and Emotion Regulation Efficacy.

Both were assessed daily on 11-point bipolar scales from -5 to +5. Perceived emotion regulation efficacy was a single item ("How well were you able to deal with your feelings today?"), and well-being the mean of two items ("How well did you feel today?" ; "How satisfied did you feel today?").

General and Interpersonal Stress.

Participants indicated daily whether they had experienced anything stressful and, if so, selected the most stressful event from a 14-item checklist of common adolescent stressors (e.g., conflict with friends or parents, exclusion, time pressure, negative feedback) plus an open-ended option; responses were recoded as a binary indicator. On days with a reported event, participants rated its intensity ("How stressful was the event for you?") from 0 (not at all) to 10 (very much). Separately, participants rated how stressful their most important daily interaction had been on the same 0-10 scale.

Racial Discrimination.

Direct discrimination was assessed daily with the item "Were you treated unfairly today?" (yes/no). Participants who answered yes selected the perceived reason(s) for this treatment from a list. Reasons referring to migration background, religion, being a person of color or not looking "typically German," or language skills (e.g., accent) were classified as racial discrimination. Vicarious discrimination was assessed with "Did you observe today how someone was treated unfairly?" (no / once / more than once), followed by the same list of reasons plus an open-text field. Both were modeled as daily dichotomous indicators (any vs. none).

Discrimination Severity.

The subjective emotional impact of discrimination was assessed with "How did the situation feel?", rated from -5 (very bad) to +5 (very good) after both the direct and the vicarious discrimination prompts. For alignment with other event and stress markers, ratings are summarized and interpreted reverse-scored so that higher values indicate a more aversive experience; this departs from the preregistered raw scaling only in sign (see

Deviations from the Preregistration).

Prejudice Attribution. Participants who indicated that they had experienced or witnessed an unfair event were asked to indicate whether they thought that the person behaving unfair did so based on prejudice, rated on a 0 (I do not believe that) to 10 (I firmly believe that) slider.

Data Analysis

Preregistered Analyses. To address RQ1.1, we conducted multilevel exploratory factor analysis (ML-EFA) to examine the factor structure of daily psychopathology facet scales, anticipating a higher-order structure reflecting six domains-internalizing, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition, anankastia, and psychoticism-based on Wright et al. (2025).

Solutions with one to seven factors at each level (49 solutions in total) were estimated with geomin rotation, and the retained solution was selected on the basis of eigenvalues, model fit, admissibility of estimates, and interpretability. Internal consistency was evaluated using McDonald's omega (ω) at both the within-and between-person levels, and intraclass correlation coefficients (ICCs) were computed at the domain level to quantify the proportion of variance attributable to each level. Preregistered hypotheses (H1-H3) were tested using dynamic structural equation modeling (DSEM), which decomposes daily variables into within-and between-person components, accommodates individual differences in reactivity via random slopes, and controls for temporal carryover via autoregressive paths. A schematic overview of the estimated model structure is displayed in Figure 1. Concurrent within-person effects (H1.1-H1.3) were tested in separate models for each psychopathology domain and predictor, regressing same-day psychopathology on direct or vicarious discrimination (H1.1), on discrimination severity (H1.2), or on general daily stress and stress severity (H1.3). Lagged effects (H2.1-H2.3) were tested in models in which psychopathology on day t + 1 was regressed on the stressor on day t and on psychopathology on day t (autoregressive effect). Each stressor entered solely as a lagged predictor, without its own autoregressive or reverse cross-lagged path (see Deviations from the Preregistration). All symptom and exposure within-person slopes were specified as random. At the between-person level (H3), we tested whether higher average discrimination or general stress exposure was associated with higher average psychopathology across the study period.

The models were estimated in Mplus version 8.7 using maximum likelihood estimation with robust standard errors (MLR); DSEM models used Bayesian estimation (ESTIMATOR = BAYES), with burn-in iterations (BITERATIONS) initially set to 1,000 and increased as needed based on convergence diagnostics (e.g., trace plots). We considered effects credible when the 95% credibility interval (CI) excluded zero. All H1-H3 models included gender and centered age as between-person covariates and centered day number and weekday/weekend as within-person covariates; covariate estimates and residual variances are fully documented in the OSF repository (https://osf.io/vptus).

Exploratory Analyses

Beyond the preregistered hypotheses, we conducted three sets of exploratory analyses, intended to generate hypotheses for future confirmatory work rather than to test directional predictions. First, we examined whether perceived prejudice attribution (0-10) moderated the within-person association between discrimination and same-day psychopathology (stressorspecific reactivity). Three further preregistered moderators (frequency, setting, and relationship to the perpetrator) could not be analyzed (see Deviations from the Preregistration). Second, because direct and vicarious discrimination were each rare, we combined them into a composite indicator and re-estimated the H1.1 and H2.1 models, increasing estimation precision while preserving the construct's breadth (combined direct and vicarious discrimination). Third, we extended the H1.1 models by adding same-day general stress as a simultaneous predictor, testing whether discrimination explained variance in same-day psychopathology beyond, and not reducible to, general daily stress (incremental effects of discrimination).

Missing Data Handling

Two sources of incomplete data must be distinguished. First, stressor intensity and severity ratings were administered only on days with a reported event; these values are structurally inapplicable rather than missing. As preregistered, they were set to zero on days without the corresponding event (i.e., no subjective intensity for general stress and the neutral scale midpoint for discrimination severity), preserving the temporal structure of the data. Second, missing diary days (nonresponse) were assumed to be missing at random (MAR) conditional on the observed data. DSEM handles such within-person missingness via a Kalman filter, retaining model-based predictions rather than discarding occasions, and accommodates unequally spaced observations via TINTERVAL (Asparouhov et al., 2018; Hamaker & Grasman, 2012); Bayesian estimation with diffuse priors yields results comparable to full-information maximum likelihood under MAR (Asparouhov & Muthén, 2010). Because MAR cannot be verified directly, we examined whether compliance was related to baseline characteristics (see Results).

Deviations From the Preregistration

Several deviations are documented here for transparency. Of the four preregistered exploratory moderators of discrimination reactivity, only prejudice attribution could be analyzed; frequency, relationship to the perpetrator, and setting were too sparsely answered (e.g., out of 184 direct ethnic-racial discriminatory events, a second same day unfair treatment was reported only on 12 person days). Incremental effects of discrimination beyond general stress were modelled only concurrently, given the sparse next-day effects in the main models. The preregistration specified a latent-propensity (probit) parameterization for the dichotomous discrimination indicators; under this specification the preregistered random slopes did not converge, because more than 80% of participants showed no within-person variation in these rare events. We therefore entered the 0/1 indicators as metric predictors, which allowed all random slopes to be retained. The preregistration further specified fully cross-lagged models. We instead modeled each stressor solely as a lagged predictor of psychopathology, without its own autoregressive or reverse cross-lagged path (and consequently without the corresponding random slopes and their between-person variances and covariances). These paths were not hypothesized, and in the fully cross-lagged probit specification the autoregressive path of the rare binary indicator produced spuriously large lagged discrimination effects, consistent with known limitations of autoregressive models for rare binary outcomes (McNeish et al., 2024). We applied the predictor-only specification to all stressors so that estimates are directly comparable across predictors. Discrimination severity was interpreted and reported reversed scored so that higher values indicate more aversive experiences, which changes only the sign of the corresponding coefficients and is in line with the interpretation of other event variables.

Results

Preliminary Analyses.

Compliance was operationalized as the proportion of completed daily surveys out of a maximum of 29 (28 diary days plus the optional baseline evening); partial entries did not occur. All 310 participants who began the diary phase provided at least four entries and were retained, yielding 8,054 person-days (of 8,990 possible)foot_0 . Participants completed on average 25.98 entries (SD = 4.54, Mdn = 28; 89.59% of possible, range = 13.79-100%); 95.48% of participants completed at least 50% and 82.26% at least 80% of surveys, meeting or exceeding typical compliance in adolescent daily-diary research on discrimination (60%-90%; Wang et al., 2026). Completion declined modestly from 94.84% on the first day to 82.26% on the last and was similar on weekdays and weekends (89.60% vs. 89.56%; see supplemental Table S1 for statistical analysis). As preregistered, day number and weekdayversus-weekend status were included as within-person covariates in all substantive models.

Across models and psychopathology domains, day number was associated with lower psychopathology (β = -.127 to -.036, all 95% CIs excluding zero) indicating small decreases in psychopathology except for antangonism, which showed no association with study day number across models (β = -.010 to .007, all 95% CIs spanning zero). The expression of antagonism, detachment and disinhibition did not differ between weekdays and weekend days (β = -.031 to .004, all 95% CIs spanning zero). Across models, anankastia (β = -.124 to -.094, all 95% CIs excluding zero) and psychoticism (β = -.033 to -.022, all 95% CIs excluding zero) were lower on weekend days compared to weekdays. This pattern was also observed for internalizing symptoms (β = -.031 to -.022, all 95% CIs excluding zero), except for the reactivity (RQ2.1) models including the event markers stress (β = -.010, 95% CI [-.031, .011]) and stress intensity (β = -.009, 95% CI [-.030, .012]).

To evaluate the plausibility of the missing-at-random (MAR) assumption, we tested whether compliance was associated with baseline characteristics (see supplemental Table S2, S3).

Compliance was unrelated to sociodemographic characteristics and to most baseline symptom scales, with the exception of baseline YSR Inattention/Hyperactivity and conduct problem scales, which was not mirrored in the PID-5BF+M Disinhibition scale. We see this as a modest departure from MAR that we note as a limitation but did not otherwise adjust for, as neither was central to our substantive hypotheses.

Event Frequencies

Discrimination. Direct racial discrimination was reported on 2.28% of completed persondays (184 of 8,054 entries), by 24.19% (n = 75) of participants at least once during the diary period (M = 2.45 days per participant who reported any, SD = 2.37, range = 1-14). Vicarious racial discrimination was reported on 2.42% of person-days (195 of 8,054 entries), by 32.58% (n = 101) of participants at least once (M = 1.93 days per participant who reported any, SD = 1.95, range = 1-14). On 0.50% of person-days (n = 40), participants reported both direct and vicarious racial discrimination 4 . Across both forms (n = 379 events), the most frequently endorsed attribution was migration background (72.03%, n = 273), followed by religion (21.11%, n = 80), language or accent (15.30%, n = 58), and being perceived as a person of color / not looking "typically German" (5.80%, n = 22).

General and Interpersonal Stressors.

At least one general stressful event was reported on 19.74% of days (1,590 of 8,054 entries), by 82.58% (n = 256) of participants at least once (M = 6.22 days per participant who reported any, SD = 5.99, range = 1-29). The most commonly endorsed stressor categories were feeling under time pressure (21.82%, n = 347), worrying about something coming up (9.25%, n = 147), and other, not prespecified stressors (17.61%, n = 280).

On days with a reported event, mean stress intensity was 6.89 (SD = 2.04) on the 0-10 scale.

Daily interpersonal stress (most important interaction) averaged 2.45 (SD = 1.68) across all person-days.

Co-Occurrence. Discrimination (direct and/or vicarious discrimination) and general stressors co-occurred on 1.28% (103 of 8,054 entries) of person-days; on 18.46% (n = 1,487 of 8,054 entries) of person-days a general stressor was reported in the absence of any discrimination, whereas discrimination was reported on 2.93% of person-days (n = 236) without reporting general stress events, underscoring the value of modeling discrimination as a stressor distinguishable from general daily stress.

Multilevel Factor Structure and Reliability (RQ1.1).

Intraclass correlations (ICCs) for the six daily psychopathology domains indicated substantial within-person variability, with 44% (antagonism) to 60% (detachment) of the variance attributable to day-to-day fluctuations (see Table 1 for domain-specific ICCs). The four broad-band domains common to contemporary were asked whether they had observed someone else being treated unfairly, followed by parallel questions. If neither applied, they were instructed to recall a general social interaction. A technical error altered this branching logic, enabling participants to answer questions regarding both personal and observed unfairness on the same day.

hierarchical taxonomies (internalizing, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition) showed withinperson variance proportions comparable to those reported by Wright et al. (2025) in their adult validation sample (~40-50%), supporting the use of this inventory for capturing daily symptom dynamics in an adolescent population. Descriptive statistics and ICCs for all daily domains are presented in Table 1.

Model fit improved systematically with increasing numbers of factors across the 49 estimated solutions (full fit statistics in supplemental Table S4). At the within-person level, five factors had eigenvalues exceeding 1.0 (ranging from 4.65 to 1.06); a sixth factor was retained on theoretical grounds, supported by a marked improvement in fit between the five-and six-factor solutions (ΔCFI = .035, ΔRMSEA = .008 for the W6B3 solution). At the between-person level, three eigenvalues exceeded 1.0 (9.56, 2.04, 1.83), supporting a more parsimonious structure. The first six within-person eigenvalues accounted for 63.7% of the within-person variance, and the first three between-person eigenvalues for 74.5% of the between-person variance. In the retained W6B3 solution, the common factors explained 47.1% of the standardized within-person variance and 70.8% of the between-person variance (mean communalities). The W6B3 solution showed excellent and fully admissible fit (CFI = .965, TLI = .934, RMSEA = .022, SRMRwithin = .011, SRMRbetween = .047) and we retained it as the primary solution, although two between-person loadings approached unity (aggression = .998, overconcern = .997). A six-factor between-person alternative (W6B6) showed marginally better fit indices (CFI = .985, TLI = .963, RMSEA = .016, SRMRwithin = .011, SRMRbetween = .017); however, several between-person loadings exceeded 1.0, indicating inadmissible estimates attributable to overfactoring. Given this admissibility violation, we did not interpret the W6B6 solution further. Subsequent validity and stressor analyses used the six daily domain scores, each computed as the mean of its three items.

Figure 2 displays the W6B3 solution with its factor loadings.

At the within-person level, six dimensions emerged, broadly corresponding to internalizing, detachment, antagonism, disinhibition, anankastia, and psychoticism. Primary loadings ranged from .35 to .86, with cross-loadings uniformly below .20. Internalizing was primarily defined by affective lability, anxiety, and separation insecurity; detachment by anhedonia, social withdrawal, and emotional detachment; antagonism by aggression, vindictiveness, and callousness; disinhibition by impulsivity, irresponsibility, and risk taking; and anankastia by perfectionism, overconcern, and work Ethic. A sixth factor was defined by unusual beliefs, distorted thinking, and perceptual Dysregulation.

At the between-person level, the W6B3 solution yielded three factors: an internalizing factor (combining anhedonia, social withdrawal, affective lability, anxiety), a broad externalizing factor (combining antagonism, disinhibition, and thought-disorder content), and an anankastia factor (combining perfectionism, work ethic, overconcern). Separation Insecurity showed crossloadings on both internalizing and externalizing, and unusual beliefs and perceptual dysregulation showed small secondary loadings on anankastia (.20 and .17).

Convergence With Baseline Self-Report Inventories (RQ1.2).

Daily psychopathology domains converged with their conceptual counterparts across both baseline inventories (supplemental Tables S5 and S6). Convergent correlations with the PID-5 were moderate in magnitude and, in most cases, the largest in their respective rows and columns: detachment (r = .45), antagonism (r = .39), anankastia (r = .37), and psychoticism (r = .34), all p < .001. Daily internalizing correlated most strongly with PID-5 Negative Affectivity (r = .40, p < .001), consistent with the conceptual alignment between these constructs. Daily disinhibition's convergent correlation (r = .35) with PID-5 Disinhibition was slightly lower than with PID-5 Antagonism (r = .37, p < .001) and was slightly exceeded, by daily internalizing's correlation with the same scale (r = .39, p < .001). Moreover, daily antagonism showed a comparably high correlation with PID-5 Detachment (r = .36), suggesting that day-level antagonism in this sample carries an interpersonal-withdrawal component. Daily Anankastia showed the strongest discriminant pattern overall-unrelated to PID-5 Negative Affectivity, Disinhibition, and Psychoticism (rs = .01-.08, all ps > .05)-though small significant correlations with PID-5 Antagonism (r = .23) and Detachment (r = .12) indicate the pattern was not entirely clean.

Convergence with the YSR DSM-oriented scales followed a parallel structure. Daily internalizing and detachment both correlated most strongly with YSR Depression and Anxiety (rs = .47-.48, all p < .001), reflecting the shared internalizing-affective content of these scales.

Daily antagonism and disinhibition correlated most strongly with YSR Conduct Problems (r = .47 and .40, respectively), and daily disinhibition additionally with YSR ADHD (r = .33). Daily psychoticism showed moderate correlations across all YSR scales (rs = .15-.27), consistent with the cross-cutting nature of psychoticism-spectrum content. Daily anankastia was essentially uncorrelated with every YSR scale (|r| ≤ .05, all ps > .05), consistent with the absence of Anankastia content in the YSR item pool and paralleling its discriminant profile on the PID-5.

Daily Convergence With Affect, Emotion Regulation Efficacy, and Well-Being (RQ1.3) Within-Person Associations.

On days when youth reported elevated psychopathology relative to their own average, they generally also reported higher negative affect (NA), lower emotion regulation efficacy and well-being (WB), and, for most domains, lower positive affect (supplemental Table S7). Associations were strongest for internalizing (NA r = .33; WB r = -.22) and detachment (NA r = .25; WB r = -.23) and smaller for antagonism, disinhibition, and psychoticism (NA rs = .18-.24); positive affect was unrelated to psychoticism and anankastia.

PREPRINT: DISCRIMINATION AND DAILY PSYCHOPATHOLOGY IN YOUTH 24

Anankastia diverged most clearly, showing small positive associations with both NA (r = .10) and emotion regulation efficacy (r = .05) and no association with well-being.

Between-Person Associations. Youth with higher average daily psychopathology reported higher average NA (rs = .33 [anankastia] to .71 [internalizing]) and, for all domains except anankastia, lower average emotion regulation efficacy (rs = -.34 to -.40) and well-being (rs = -.37 to -.42). Associations with positive affect were weak and largely nonsignificant.

Anankastia again diverged, correlating only with NA.

Dynamic Associations Between Daily Stressors and Psychopathology (H1-H3)

Concurrent Within-Person Associations (H1). Supporting H1.1, days marked by direct racial discrimination were accompanied by elevated psychopathology across all domains except anankastia (βs = .07-.11, all 95% CIs excluding zero), with the largest effect for antagonism (β = .11, 95% CI [.05, .16]; Table 2). Supporting H1.2, greater perceived severity of discrimination was associated with higher same-day symptom levels across the same five domains (βs = .06-.10), again with the largest effect for antagonism (β = .10, 95% CI [.04, .16]) and no effect for anankastia (β = .00, 95% CI [-.03, .03]). Supporting H1.3, daily stressful events were associated with elevations across all six domains, with the largest effects for internalizing (β = .22, 95% CI [.20, .25]), disinhibition (β = .15), and detachment (β = .14), and the smallest for antagonism and anankastia (both β = .06).

General and social stress severity showed credible positive associations across all six domains, with social stress severity yielding the largest effects throughout (Table 2).

Vicarious discrimination showed a narrower pattern (Table 3). Antagonism was elevated on days with any vicarious discrimination (β = .08, 95% CI [.04, .12]) and with greater vicarious severity (β = .06, 95% CI [.01, .11]). One smaller effect emerged-psychoticism was elevated on days with any vicarious discrimination (β = .03, 95% CI [.01, .07]) with all other CIs including zero.

Incremental Effects of Discrimination Beyond General Stress (Exploratory).

Modeling same-day occurrence of discrimination and general stress simultaneously, discrimination retained independent associations with internalizing (β = .04, 95% CI [.01, .08]), disinhibition (β = .05, 95% CI [.01, .08]), psychoticism (β = .06, 95% CI [.02, .09]), and antagonism (β = .11, 95% CI [.06, .15]); detachment and anankastia showed no incremental effect (βs = .02 to .03, CIs spanning zero). General stress was the stronger predictor in every domain except antagonism, where discrimination's effect exceeded it more than twofold (βdiscrimination= .11 vs. βstress= .05).

This asymmetry corroborates the main analyses in identifying antagonism as the domain most specifically tied to discrimination rather than to daily stress in general.

Lagged Within-Person Associations (H2).

Contrary to H2.1, lagged effects of direct discrimination were not credible in any domain (all CIs including zero; Table 2). H2.2 was likewise not supported for direct discrimination: the only credible lagged severity effect ran opposite to prediction, with greater severity of direct discrimination on day t predicting lower, not higher, antagonism on day t + 1 (β = -.04, 95% CI [-.08, -.01]). H2.3 was not supported either; the lagged associations of general stress were domainspecific and consistently negative. General daily stress predicted lower next-day antagonism, disinhibition, and psychoticism (βs = -.03 to -.05), and general stress severity showed a similar but broader pattern, credible for internalizing, detachment, antagonism, and psychoticism (βs = -.03 to -.05) but not disinhibition or anankastia. Social stress severity showed no credible lagged associations in any domain (Table 2). Because all models adjust for same-day psychopathology, these negative coefficients indicate that symptom levels on the day after a stressful day fell below what the autoregressive effect alone would predict-a recovery rather than a carryover pattern.

Lagged effects of vicarious discrimination were sparse (Table 3). Dichotomous vicarious discrimination did not predict next-day psychopathology in any domain. In the direction hypothesized under H2.2, however, greater vicarious discrimination severity on day t predicted higher internalizing (β = .05, 95% CI [.02, .07]) and detachment (β = .04, 95% CI [.01, .07]) on day t + 1, with no other credible effects.

Between-Person Associations (H3).

Supporting H3, individuals reporting more frequent or more severe discrimination or general stress showed higher average psychopathology (Table 4). Direct discrimination frequency was positively associated with all six domains (rs = .25-.37), most strongly for psychoticism and antagonism (rs = .37), and vicarious discrimination frequency showed a comparable pattern across all six domains (rs = .22-.34). Average severity of direct discrimination was positively associated with all domains (rs = .12-.22), but not with disinhibition; average vicarious severity was associated with internalizing (r = .28) and detachment (r = .27) only.

General stress frequency and severity showed positive associations for internalizing (rs = .31/.35), detachment (.17/.20), and anankastia (.13/.14), but not antagonism, disinhibition or psychoticism. Social stress severity showed the strongest and most consistent associations, credibly different from zero across all six domains and ranging from r = .29 (anankastia) to r = .72 (internalizing).

Exploratory Analyses.

PREPRINT: DISCRIMINATION AND DAILY PSYCHOPATHOLOGY IN YOUTH 27

Stressor-Specific Reactivity. Whether youth attributed the discriminatory event to prejudice did not moderate the association between direct (βs = -.03 to .00) or vicarious (βs =.00 to .04) discrimination and any daily psychopathology domain (all CIs spanning zero).

Combined Direct and Vicarious Discrimination.

A composite indicator of direct and/or vicarious discrimination reproduced the main pattern: same-day elevations in internalizing, detachment, disinhibition, and psychoticism (βs = .03-.06), the largest effect for antagonism (β = .11, 95% CI [.06, .15]), no association with anankastia, and no next-day effects in any domain (all CIs spanning zero).

Discussion

Ethnoracially minoritized youth navigate the developmentally sensitive window of adolescence alongside recurrent discrimination, from microaggressions to institutional barriers (Civitillo & Jugert, 2024; Uhlhaas et al., 2023). Integrating stress process theory with transdiagnostic models, the present study examined daily discrimination and psychopathology via a multilevel daily symptom inventory and within-person associations between discrimination and symptom fluctuations. The inventory captured a robust six-factor structure within persons that converged with established baseline measures, though the between-person structure resolved into three broader spectra. Discrimination and general stress both predicted same-day symptom elevations, and discrimination showed incremental associations beyond general stress for internalizing, antagonism, disinhibition, and psychoticism -strongest for antagonism. Next-day associations were largely absent; where present, they mostly indicated recovery rather than carryover.

Multilevel Factor Structure.

Six dimensions with minimal cross-loadings emerged within persons, closely mirroring the structure reported by Wright et al. (2025). At the between-person level, two solutions fit adequately, but only the more parsimonious three-factor solution (W6B3) was fully admissible; the six-factor alternative yielded out-of-range loadings characteristic of overfactoring. We therefore read its marginally superior fit as the expected statistical consequence of extracting additional factors rather than as evidence of a more veridical structure. The three retained factors reconstructed broadband, HiTOP-like spectra: a combined internalizing/detachment distress factor, a broad externalizing factor subsuming antagonistic, disinhibited, and psychoticism-related content, and a distinct anankastia factor. This pattern is consonant with Hankin et al.'s (2026) more general finding that the broadband tier of the hierarchy is its most stable and replicable level across informants and developmental stages, although the content of their three spectra differs from that observed here. Within persons, in contrast, the six-factor structure replicated cleanly and closely paralleled the between-person spectra recovered in adult samples (Wright et al., 2025). We interpret this within/between asymmetry as consistent with the principle of non-ergodicity (Molenaar, 2004), according to which state-and trait-level covariation need not coincide. Internalizing, detachment, and anankastia corresponded most clearly across levels, each anchoring a recognizable counterpart at both levels of analysis. Even here the correspondence was imperfect: Separation Insecurity cross-loaded onto both between-person factors, functioning as an interstitial facet at the trait level. Antagonism and Psychoticism diverged most substantially, plausibly because their daily expression is the most context-dependent of the six domains. A hostile remark or an unusual thought is a discrete, situationally triggered event, whereas the corresponding trait-level expression may not lend itself to stable self-attribution or may instead collapse into a broader externalizing liability.

PREPRINT: DISCRIMINATION AND DAILY PSYCHOPATHOLOGY IN YOUTH 29

Should this pattern replicate, it would suggest that the correspondence between withinand between-person structure depends on how context-bound a domain's expression is, rather than being a fixed property of psychopathology. Such asymmetry may likewise prove more pronounced among adolescents than adults, insofar as between-person report requires abstracting stable trait attributions-a capacity still consolidating across adolescence-whereas withinperson report remains anchored to concrete, recent experience. Structural correspondence across development is, in this regard, more often assumed than empirically demonstrated in youth research (Rescorla et al., 2007), rendering this a hypothesis that intensive longitudinal designs employing diverse samples are well positioned to test.

Finally, we remain cautious about how far these structural results address the concern that taxonomies validated outside minoritized samples risk mistaking discrimination-related adaptation for pathology. Recovering the expected structure, together with its covariation with same-day affect (RQ1.3), establishes that the measure functions coherently in this population-a precondition for use. Structural correspondence, however, does not speak to whether elevated scores reflect distress or adaptation, a question of score meaning rather than item covariance (He & Li, 2021; Rodriguez-Seijas et al., 2023). Future work could test this directly via differential item functioning analysis, which has identified group-specific item functioning in personalitydisorder criteria by sexual orientation (Asadi et al., 2024) and could analogously establish whether antagonism or detachment items carry equivalent diagnostic meaning for youth with high versus low discrimination exposure.

Convergent and Discriminant Validity.

Daily domains converged moderately with their conceptually matched PID-5 and YSR counterparts, most cleanly for internalizing and detachment. Antagonism and disinhibition showed broader overlap: daily antagonism correlated quite strongly with PID-5 Detachment, and daily disinhibition's convergence with its PID-5 counterpart was exceeded by PID-5 Antagonism and daily internalizing. Both converged most strongly with YSR Conduct Problems. Conceivably, adolescent antagonism and disinhibition are embedded in relational conflict and emotionally charged peer and family contexts that have not yet differentiated into circumscribed trait categories. Psychoticism correlated moderately with all YSR scales, consistent with the boundary-spanning role theorized for psychoticism-spectrum content within HiTOP or with the same entangled daily expression observed for antagonism and disinhibition. Daily anankastia converged with PID-5 Anankastia but not with the YSR, which lacks Anankastia content, and was largely unrelated to distress-laden scales; its small correlations with PID-5 Antagonism and Detachment plausibly reflect rigid, rule-bound behavior that can appear interpersonally difficult or withdrawn. This reinforces its status as a comparatively distinct, non-distress-laden domain.

Affective and Functional Convergence. Rather than converging uniformly with affective experience, the six domains arrayed along a gradient of affective saturation.

Internalizing and detachment occupied one pole, tracking negative affect, well-being, and regulation efficacy most closely, as expected of domains substantially constituted by felt distress. Antagonism, disinhibition, and psychoticism assumed an intermediate position, showing the same directional pattern at attenuated magnitude; their defining content is behavioral and cognitive rather than affective and need not entail subjective distress. Anankastia anchored the opposing pole, associating minimally with well-being or positive affect at either level, consistent with Wright et al.'s (2025) characterization of it as least saturated with affective distress.

Anankastia was nonetheless the theoretically informative exception, because it was not merely affectively inert: a small positive association with negative affect co-occurred with a small positive association with perceived regulation efficacy, such that days of heightened perfectionism brought both greater strain and greater regulatory competence. This dissociation is consistent with established distinctions between adaptive and maladaptive perfectionism, and it carries a broader implication: symptom elevation that coincides with intact functioning cannot be read as a self-evident index of distress, because its significance depends on domain-specific content. This affective patterning, moreover, held at both within-and between-person levels, in contrast to the structural asymmetry documented under RQ1.1. Symptom organization, not experiential correlates, differed across levels, indicating that the within/between divergence is specific to covariance structure rather than reflecting a general discontinuity between daily states and trait-level standing.

Reactivity, Carryover, and the Specificity of Discrimination. Consistent with H1, direct discrimination was associated with same-day elevations across most domains and vicarious discrimination with elevated antagonism and psychoticism, with antagonism showing the largest effect in both cases. Greater perceived severity of discrimination was likewise associated with higher same-day symptoms; because severity was coded at the neutral midpoint on days without discrimination, however, this association is conditional on occurrence and does not reflect a dose-response relationship. The incremental models tested whether discrimination constitutes a distinct source of burden or merely one instance of daily stress. Modeled simultaneously, both retained independent associations with same-day symptoms across four domains, indicating that neither is reducible to the other; the normative stressors of adolescence thus do not exhaust what minoritized adolescents must navigate, as discrimination is superimposed upon them. Because the present sample was minoritized in its entirety, this additivity is established within, not across, persons: the design supports the conclusion that these burdens compound, but not that they exceed those borne by non-minoritized peers. Within this additive structure, general stress constituted the stronger predictor in every domain but one; only for Antagonism did discrimination's effect exceed it, by more than twofold (βdiscrimination = .11 vs. βstress = .05)-the sole domain better accounted for by identity-relevant treatment than by daily burden in general.

Lagged associations largely did not follow the hypothesized pattern. Contrary to H2.1, direct discrimination on day t was unrelated to next-day psychopathology in any domain. Where lagged effects emerged, they were small and predominantly negative: general stress and stress severity on day t predicted lower next-day symptoms in several domains, and more aversive direct discrimination predicted lower next-day antagonism. Because these models adjust for same-day symptoms, this pattern suggests recovery rather than carryover, plausibly reflecting same-day recruitment of coping and social support (Brody et al., 2006; Seaton & Douglass, 2014). The only effects in the hypothesized direction concerned vicarious discrimination: more aversive witnessed discrimination predicted higher next-day internalizing and detachment.

Because the dichotomous vicarious indicator showed no such effect and severity largely mirrors occurrence (see Limitations), these effects are fragile; given their small magnitude and the number of tests conducted, they warrant replication.

The convergence of these properties upon a single domain invites a clinically grounded interpretation. Appraisal-theoretic accounts differentiate anger, arising from attributions of otheragency and injustice, from sadness and withdrawal, arising from appraisals of irrevocable loss (Lazarus, 1991; Smith & Ellsworth, 1985); discrimination constitutes a prototypical injustice appraisal. Insofar as antagonistic content partly indexes anger-adjacent states, an acute same-day surge in Antagonism that exceeds what general stress produces is what such accounts anticipate. This surge did not persist, however: greater severity of discrimination predicted lower, not higher, Antagonism on the following day. Read jointly with the incremental pattern (a domain indexing general distress should track general stress, as the remaining domains did), this suggests that elevated daily antagonism reflects not stable antagonistic psychopathology but a transient, situationally bound response to identity-based treatment, as identity-relevant stressor accounts predict (Major & O'Brien, 2005; Ong et al., 2009; Pearlin, 1989; Tajfel & Turner, 1979). Such a response may take the form of heightened guardedness toward strangers, defensive responding to ambiguous remarks, avoidance of particular settings, or more confrontational engagement on the day of a discriminatory encounter, followed by recovery. Construing such elevations as trait-like hostility would therefore be misleading (Kingston et al., 2023), a risk with empirical precedent: sexual and gender minority individuals endorse antagonism and detachment indicators at elevated rates as a function of group status alone (Asadi et al., 2024), and antagonisticexternalizing items likewise capture responses to discriminatory treatment in racialized groups, so that identical scores do not carry identical meaning across groups (Rodriguez-Seijas et al., 2023). Two qualifications apply. First, our measures do not license the inference from domain content to emotional state; distinguishing anger from other antagonistic content would require discrete-emotion items. Second, the incremental specificity of antagonism emerged from exploratory models. We therefore advance this reading as a hypothesis, to be tested in a replication that assesses anger separately from other negative emotions.

Between-Person Associations. Supporting H3, youth reporting more frequent discrimination or general stress also reported higher average psychopathology, with social stress severity showing the broadest and strongest associations. Thus, although discrimination did not carry over from one day to the next, chronic exposure tracked meaningfully with average symptom burden, consistent with stress process theory's premise that persistent exposure and recurrent daily stressors are reciprocally entangled (Lazarus & Folkman, 1984; Pearlin, 1989). Its effects may accumulate over longer timescales rather than propagate through discrete day-to-day sequences, although these person-level associations cannot establish direction.

Limitations. Several limitations qualify these findings. Discrimination was rarely endorsed on any given day, limiting power for lagged effects and raising the risk of false positives across the many domains and predictors tested. Moreover, individual differences in discrimination reactivity (random slopes) are informed only by the roughly one quarter of participants who reported any discrimination, so that these estimates and any moderation of them should be regarded as exploratory. Entering the dichotomous discrimination indicators as metric predictors rather than as latent propensities, as preregistered, was necessary to retain random slopes, but it means that standardized discrimination coefficients are scaled by the observed indicator and are not directly comparable to those of the preregistered probit specification. The incremental specificity of antagonism, though theoretically coherent, emerged from exploratory models and requires confirmatory replication. Daily sampling resolution may be too coarse to capture shorter-timescale or event-specific carryover, and the between-person structural analyses (RQ1.1) rest on far fewer units than the within-person analyses, somewhat limiting their stability and comparability.

Because severity ratings were, as preregistered, set to the neutral midpoint on days without discrimination, severity scores were largely determined by whether discrimination occurred, both within persons and in person-level averages. The severity analyses (H1.2, H2.2, and the severity components of H3) cannot separate how aversive an experience was from whether it occurred.

All measures relied on adolescent self-report, including discrimination classification-a deliberate choice, given that perceived discrimination is the theoretically relevant construct (Pascoe & Smart Richman, 2009). This is particularly consequential here: discrimination that is chronic or embedded in normalized social routines may become harder to recognize and label as such over time, such that habituated individuals might underreport experiences that did occurre, rendering our estimates potentially conservative-a possibility future work combining informant-reports could address.

Finally, generalizability is constrained by recruitment from a single German region (the Wuppertal area of North Rhine-Westphalia, with an above-average share of residents with a migration background) and in-person eligibility requirements, and compliance was modestly related to baseline YSR Conduct Problems and Inattention/Hyperactivity. More fundamentally, the present inventory, though adapted for this population, remains an etic application of a structure built and validated largely on other populations; future work might instead pursue a more emic approach, building item content and constructs from the ground up within this specific population and context (Rodriguez-Seijas et al., 2023).

Conclusion

This study extends hierarchical models of daily psychopathology to a population and context in which they have rarely been tested. In ethnoracially minoritized adolescents in Germany, racial discrimination emerged as an acute, identity-relevant stressor whose same-day associations with symptoms, most distinctively with antagonism, were not reducible to general daily stress and whose occurrence did not carry over to the following day. Daily symptom elevations in this population, particularly antagonistic ones, may therefore index situationally PREPRINT: DISCRIMINATION AND DAILY PSYCHOPATHOLOGY IN YOUTH 36 bound responses to identity-based treatment rather than stable psychopathology, a distinction 782 with direct implications for how such elevations are assessed and interpreted in clinical practice. The study design, hypotheses, and analysis plan were preregistered prior to data analysis (https://osf.io/79j8k). Deviations from the preregistration are documented and justified in the Method section (see Deviations From the Preregistration).

Ethical approval. The study was approved by the Ethics Committee of the Bergische Universität Wuppertal (ref. no. SK/AE 240113) and conducted in accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki. All participants, and a legal guardian of participants under 16, provided informed consent.

For

RQ1.2 and RQ1.3, multilevel modeling (MLM) was used to examine associations between daily psychopathology and both baseline and momentary measures, exploiting the hierarchical structure of daily observations nested within individuals. At the between-person level (RQ1.2), convergent validity was assessed by correlating participants' mean daily PREPRINT: DISCRIMINATION AND DAILY PSYCHOPATHOLOGY IN YOUTH 15 psychopathology scores with established baseline measures (e.g., YSR, PID-5). At the withinperson level (RQ1.3), daily covariation between psychopathology and time-varying constructsnegative and positive affect, perceived emotion regulation efficacy, and well-being-was examined in a separate single model comprising a two-level (day-within-person) structure.

Born in Germany comprised yes (n = 228) and no (n = 82). Only categories represented in the analytic sample were included in the ANOVAs 1080 and are reported here.

1103

NA = negative affect; PA = positive affect; Eff = emotion regulation efficacy; WB = well-being.

1104 *p < .05. **p < .01. ***p < .001.

DSEM models were estimated on the TINTERVAL time grid, which comprises 8,989 daily observations across the 310 participants; of these, 8,054 person-days were completed, and the remaining days were handled by the Kalman filter rather than discarded. The table notes therefore report 8,989 observations.
